    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F32.0, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess the short version is that I’m not feeling quite like myself lately. I’m an elementary—well, middle school now—teacher, and the district recently put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on me on top of my regular teaching responsibilities. And I can do it, technically, I mean I’m still showing up and getting through the school day, but it’s been sitting on me in a way I didn’t expect.\n\nI feel wound up a lot, and discouraged, which is not really how I usually am about work. I’ve always been the kind of person who handles lesson plans and emails and all the little follow-up things pretty automatically, but lately I keep putting them off. I’ll look at my inbox or know I need to adjust a lesson plan, and I just sort of avoid it, which is frustrating because that’s not like me. So I came in because I don’t want it to keep getting worse, and I’m trying to understand what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things, even when there isn’t a clear reason, or feeling like you can’t quite control your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it comes completely out of nowhere, if that’s what you mean. It feels pretty tied to this work situation—ever since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto my regular teaching load. I’m still functioning, I’m still going in and teaching and getting through the day, but I feel wound up in a way that doesn’t feel like me.\n\nAnd it’s discouraging, honestly, because I’m used to being pretty steady with work things. Lesson plans, emails, all the little follow-up pieces—I’ve handled those for years without making a big production of it. Lately I’ll know I need to sit down and revise something or respond to messages, and I just put it off. Then I feel worse because I’m not used to avoiding those things. So the anxiety is there, yes, but it feels like it’s wrapped around this added responsibility more than just random worrying for no reason.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms, like restlessness, fatigue, or muscle tension, especially when you're thinking about these work responsibilities?\n\nPatient: I mean, the main thing I notice is feeling wound up, yes, especially when I’m thinking about that rollout or all the pieces attached to it. It’s not like I’m falling apart during the school day—I’m still there, still teaching, still doing what has to be done in front of the students—but inside I feel tense and kind of discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me.\n\nIt’s mostly tied to the extra responsibility the district put on me, the bilingual social-emotional learning program on top of my regular teaching. I’ll think, okay, I need to adjust these lesson plans, I need to answer those emails, I need to stay on top of the rollout materials, and instead of just doing it like I normally would, I put it off. And then that makes me feel even more wound up, because I’m used to handling those things without a second thought. So yes, physically or emotionally—maybe both—I feel keyed up around it, even though I can still get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, of course I have ordinary life things I think about, like anyone does, but what brought me in is very specifically this work situation. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, it’s like I can still get through the school day and do what I need to do in front of the students, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more that the work feeling follows me home, if that makes sense. I’ll be thinking about lesson plans I haven’t adjusted or emails I haven’t answered, and those are things I used to handle almost automatically. Now I catch myself putting them off, and then I feel worse because I know I’m avoiding them. But I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly anxious about my relationships or my health or every little everyday responsibility. It’s really centered around this added responsibility at school.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting the same satisfaction or enjoyment out of things you usually like to do, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost enjoyment in things, exactly. I mean, I haven’t sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t like this anymore,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that when I’m outside of work, I’m often still sort of mentally tangled up in the work piece, so I may not settle into things as easily.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to overstate it. If I’m doing something I usually enjoy, I can still enjoy it. It’s just that sometimes there’s this background noise in my head about what I haven’t finished or what I need to respond to, and that makes it harder to feel fully relaxed. So… not no enjoyment, really. More like my attention gets pulled back toward school.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or losing interest in things more often, even small things that used to feel rewarding or give you a sense of accomplishment?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like I’ve lost interest across the board, but yes, I do feel more discouraged than I’m used to, and it’s mostly around this work situation. I came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate—on top of my regular teaching—I can still get through the day and do what I need to do, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that just isn’t like me.\n\nAnd the part that bothers me is that small work things that used to give me a sense of, “Okay, that’s done, I’m on top of it,” don’t feel that way right now because I’m putting them off. Lesson plans, emails, rollout materials—those are things I used to handle without a second thought. Now I’ll know I need to do them, and I’ll avoid them, and then I feel worse because that’s not how I usually operate. So it’s not that nothing feels rewarding anymore, exactly. It’s more that this added responsibility has made me feel kind of stuck and discouraged, and even little accomplishments at work don’t come as naturally as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood most of the time, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Not most of the time, no. I mean, I do feel discouraged, and more than I’m used to, but when I really look at it, it keeps circling back to the school situation. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I can still get through the day and be present for the students, but I feel wound up and kind of defeated in a way that doesn’t feel like me.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work, I don’t think I’m just sitting in a low mood all the time. It’s more like work intrudes. I’ll be at home and suddenly remember lesson plans I haven’t adjusted, or emails I haven’t answered, and then I get that heavy, discouraged feeling because those are things I used to handle without much thought. Now I’m putting them off, and that makes me feel worse about myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m low even completely separate from work. It’s more that this work stress has started spilling over, and I’m having trouble shaking it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to control your worry, even when you try, or noticed that it’s starting to interfere with your ability to focus or get things done outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the part I’m having the hardest time admitting, honestly. I’m still functioning. I’m still showing up, still teaching, still doing the day-to-day things I absolutely have to do. So from the outside, I probably look like I’m managing.\n\nBut I’m not managing the way I normally do. I’ve pushed back planning deadlines, or just sort of missed the internal deadlines I would usually set for myself. And there have been a few times where colleagues have had to remind me about things—small tasks, planning pieces, materials, follow-ups—that I used to just take care of reliably. That feels very unlike me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m completely unable to work or anything like that. It’s more that the worry keeps taking up space in my head, and then I’ll avoid starting something, or I’ll jump between things and not finish them. Then someone checks in or reminds me, and I feel embarrassed because I’m usually the person who has those things handled. So yes, it has started interfering, even if I’m still keeping the basic responsibilities going.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy don’t feel rewarding or pleasurable anymore, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that broad way you’re asking. I mean, I have moments where I’m distracted, so something I’d normally enjoy might not fully land because my mind drifts back to school. But I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone from things in general.\n\nIf I’m really not thinking about work, I can still enjoy things. It’s more that getting myself fully away from thinking about work has been harder. So the activity itself isn’t the problem, if that makes sense. It’s not like I sit down to do something I normally like and feel absolutely nothing. It’s more that I’ll be trying to relax and then a thought about some task or email or planning piece pops in, and it sort of interrupts the enjoyment.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed that everything outside of work feels unrewarding. It’s more tied to whether I can actually get my head out of work mode.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a lasting loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a lasting loss, no. At least, not the way I understand what you’re asking. I still do have interest in things I normally enjoy, and if I’m actually able to be present with them, they can still feel good.\n\nIt’s more that my attention gets pulled away, if that makes sense. Like I might start doing something I usually like and then I realize I’m half there, half somewhere else mentally. But it doesn’t feel like the enjoyment itself is gone. It feels more like I’m not always giving myself enough mental space to enjoy it.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a steady, across-the-board loss of interest or pleasure when work isn’t on my mind. If I’m truly not thinking about work, I can still enjoy things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood even when you’re not thinking about work or any of the added responsibilities at school?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I feel low in a completely separate, out-of-nowhere way. It feels tied to all of this school responsibility, even when I’m not actively sitting there thinking, “Oh, the rollout, the emails, the lesson plans.” It’s like the pressure has followed me around.\n\nEver since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt… wound up, and honestly pretty discouraged in a way that doesn’t feel like me. I still get through the day. I’m not falling apart in front of my class or anything. But there’s this heaviness or discouragement that’s been there because I know I’m not keeping up the way I usually do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off things like lesson plans or emails—things I used to just handle without a second thought—and that makes me feel worse about myself. So yes, I guess I do feel down at times, but I see it as connected to this added responsibility and the stress around school, not like a general low mood that’s totally unrelated to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, even temporarily?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost interest in almost all activities. Even temporarily, I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nThere are times when I sort of don’t feel like doing much, but when I really think about it, it’s usually because I’m preoccupied or tired from having my mind on school things. It’s not that the activities themselves suddenly seem meaningless or like I don’t care about them anymore. If I can settle into something, I can still enjoy it.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not almost all activities. It’s more like my attention gets hijacked, rather than my interest disappearing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not thinking about work, things you used to enjoy feel less satisfying or take more effort to get into?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in the way you mean. I mean, I can think of moments where something takes me longer to settle into, but I don’t know that it’s because the thing itself feels less satisfying. It’s more like I’m a little… distracted or not fully shifted out of “school mode,” even if I’m not consciously thinking through work.\n\nLike, if I sit down to do something I normally like, I might need a few minutes to actually feel present. But once I’m there, it can still feel enjoyable. So I wouldn’t say the enjoyment is gone or that things consistently feel dull or empty. It’s more that I don’t always transition into them as easily right now.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is: not really less satisfying, no. Maybe sometimes a little more effort to get into, but not because I’ve stopped caring about them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like nothing gives you pleasure or interest, even things you normally enjoy, and this lasts for most of the day, even when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in that “nothing gives me pleasure” kind of way, and not for most of a day.\n\nI mean, there are stretches where I’m kind of dulled out or I’m not as quick to reach for something enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like I might not immediately think, “Oh, I want to do this nice thing,” because my mind feels crowded or I’m just not very settled. But if I’m actually doing something I normally like, I can still get something out of it. It may take me a little while to shift gears, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is absent.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed whole days where everything feels pointless or flat even when work isn’t on my mind. It’s more uneven than that, and it still feels connected to being distracted or weighed down by the school situation rather than losing interest in life in general.\n\nDoctor: Outside of thinking about work or the added responsibilities, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even about small or everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I do worry, but it feels pretty tied to the work situation and the extra responsibilities, not like I’m just anxious about everything across the board.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or running errands or talking to someone about something unrelated, I don’t usually find myself suddenly worrying about random small things for no reason. It’s more that if something reminds me of school, or I start thinking about what still needs to be handled, then my mind can start going. But outside of that, I’m not generally walking around feeling anxious most days about ordinary little things.\n\nI mean, I may get a little tense the way anyone does if there’s traffic or I’m running late or something, but I wouldn’t call that this constant worry. So… no, not most days about everyday things apart from work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I think I’d answer that pretty much the same way, though I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to sound like I’m dismissing it. I do get tense, but I don’t really notice myself being anxious or on edge in a general way when work isn’t somewhere in the picture.

It’s more that if something reminds me of school, or I start thinking about what still needs to be handled, then my mind can start going. But outside of that, I’m not generally walking around feeling anxious most days about ordinary little things.\n\nI mean, I may get a little tense the way anyone does if there’s traffic or I’m running late or something, but I wouldn’t call that this constant worry. So… no, not most days about everyday things apart from work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities, like in general or during routine daily activities?\n\nPatient: I think I’d answer that pretty much the same way, though I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to sound like I’m dismissing it. I do get tense, but I don’t really notice myself being anxious or on edge in a general way when work isn’t somewhere in the picture.\n\nLike if I’m just doing normal things—folding laundry, going to the store, making dinner, driving somewhere familiar—I’m not usually sitting there with this background hum of worry for no clear reason. If I feel keyed up, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to school, or something reminds me of what I need to deal with, or I realize I forgot to check something. Then I can feel that little surge of tension.\n\nBut if it’s truly not on my mind, no, I don’t think I’m generally anxious during routine activities. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I mean, I can feel kind of low or discouraged at times, but when I really think about what you’re asking—most of the day, more days than not, and not connected to thinking about work or all the added responsibilities—I don’t think that fits.

It’s more like my mood dips when something brings the situation back up, or when I’m mentally going over everything and feeling worn down by it. But if I’m away from it, or absorbed in something else, I’m not generally just low all day for no reason. I wouldn’t say I’m cheerful all the time or anything, but it doesn’t feel like this steady low mood that’s there regardless of what I’m thinking about.

So… no, I haven’t noticed feeling down most of the day on most days outside of that context.

It’s more that the worry keeps taking up space in my head, and then I’ll avoid starting something, or I’ll jump between things and not finish them. Then someone checks in or reminds me, and I feel embarrassed because I’m usually the person who has those things handled. So yes, it has started interfering, even if I’m still keeping the basic responsibilities going.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy don’t feel rewarding or pleasurable anymore, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that broad way you’re asking. I mean, I have moments where I’m distracted, so something I’d normally enjoy might not fully land because my mind drifts back to school. But I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone from things in general.\n\nIf I’m really not thinking about work, I can still enjoy things. It’s more that getting myself fully away from thinking about work has been harder. So the activity itself isn’t the problem, if that makes sense. It’s not like I sit down to do something I normally like and feel absolutely nothing. It’s more that I’ll be trying to relax and then a thought about some task or email or planning piece pops in, and it sort of interrupts the enjoyment.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed that everything outside of work feels unrewarding. It’s more tied to whether I can actually get my head out of work mode.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a lasting loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a lasting loss, no. At least, not the way I understand what you’re asking. I still do have interest in things I normally enjoy, and if I’m actually able to be present with them, they can still feel good.\n\nIt’s more that my attention gets pulled away, if that makes sense. Like I might start doing something I usually like and then I realize I’m half there, half somewhere else mentally. But it doesn’t feel like the enjoyment itself is gone. It feels more like I’m not always giving myself enough mental space to enjoy it.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a steady, across-the-board loss of interest or pleasure when work isn’t on my mind. If I’m truly not thinking about work, I can still enjoy things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood even when you’re not thinking about work or any of the added responsibilities at school?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I feel low in a completely separate, out-of-nowhere way. It feels tied to all of this school responsibility, even when I’m not actively sitting there thinking, “Oh, the rollout, the emails, the lesson plans.” It’s like the pressure has followed me around.\n\nEver since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt… wound up, and honestly pretty discouraged in a way that doesn’t feel like me. I still get through the day. I’m not falling apart in front of my class or anything. But there’s this heaviness or discouragement that’s been there because I know I’m not keeping up the way I usually do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off things like lesson plans or emails—things I used to just handle without a second thought—and that makes me feel worse about myself. So yes, I guess I do feel down at times, but I see it as connected to this added responsibility and the stress around school, not like a general low mood that’s totally unrelated to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, even temporarily?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost interest in almost all activities. Even temporarily, I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nThere are times when I sort of don’t feel like doing much, but when I really think about it, it’s usually because I’m preoccupied or tired from having my mind on school things. It’s not that the activities themselves suddenly seem meaningless or like I don’t care about them anymore. If I can settle into something, I can still enjoy it.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not almost all activities. It’s more like my attention gets hijacked, rather than my interest disappearing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not thinking about work, things you used to enjoy feel less satisfying or take more effort to get into?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in the way you mean. I mean, I can think of moments where something takes me longer to settle into, but I don’t know that it’s because the thing itself feels less satisfying. It’s more like I’m a little… distracted or not fully shifted out of “school mode,” even if I’m not consciously thinking through work.\n\nLike, if I sit down to do something I normally like, I might need a few minutes to actually feel present. But once I’m there, it can still feel enjoyable. So I wouldn’t say the enjoyment is gone or that things consistently feel dull or empty. It’s more that I don’t always transition into them as easily right now.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is: not really less satisfying, no. Maybe sometimes a little more effort to get into, but not because I’ve stopped caring about them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like nothing gives you pleasure or interest, even things you normally enjoy, and this lasts for most of the day, even when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in that “nothing gives me pleasure” kind of way, and not for most of a day.\n\nI mean, there are stretches where I’m kind of dulled out or I’m not as quick to reach for something enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like I might not immediately think, “Oh, I want to do this nice thing,” because my mind feels crowded or I’m just not very settled. But if I’m actually doing something I normally like, I can still get something out of it. It may take me a little while to shift gears, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is absent.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed whole days where everything feels pointless or flat even when work isn’t on my mind. It’s more uneven than that, and it still feels connected to being distracted or weighed down by the school situation rather than losing interest in life in general.\n\nDoctor: Outside of thinking about work or the added responsibilities, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even about small or everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I do worry, but it feels pretty tied to the work situation and the extra responsibilities, not like I’m just anxious about everything across the board.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or running errands or talking to someone about something unrelated, I don’t usually find myself suddenly worrying about random small things for no reason. It’s more that if something reminds me of school, or I start thinking about what still needs to be handled, then my mind can start going. But outside of that, I’m not generally walking around feeling anxious most days about ordinary little things.\n\nI mean, I may get a little tense the way anyone does if there’s traffic or I’m running late or something, but I wouldn’t call that this constant worry. So… no, not most days about everyday things apart from work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities, like in general or during routine daily activities?\n\nPatient: I think I’d answer that pretty much the same way, though I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to sound like I’m dismissing it. I do get tense, but I don’t really notice myself being anxious or on edge in a general way when work isn’t somewhere in the picture.\n\nLike if I’m just doing normal things—folding laundry, going to the store, making dinner, driving somewhere familiar—I’m not usually sitting there with this background hum of worry for no clear reason. If I feel keyed up, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to school, or something reminds me of what I need to deal with, or I realize I forgot to check something. Then I can feel that little surge of tension.\n\nBut if it’s truly not on my mind, no, I don’t think I’m generally anxious during routine activities. I might be tired or distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as being on edge all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood for most of the day, more days than not, even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I mean, I can feel kind of low or discouraged at times, but when I really think about what you’re asking—most of the day, more days than not, and not connected to thinking about work or all the added responsibilities—I don’t think that fits.\n\nIt’s more like my mood dips when something brings the situation back up, or when I’m mentally going over everything and feeling worn down by it. But if I’m away from it, or absorbed in something else, I’m not generally just low all day for no reason. I wouldn’t say I’m cheerful all the time or anything, but it doesn’t feel like this steady low mood that’s there regardless of what I’m thinking about.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed feeling down most of the day on most days outside of that context.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really with no reason at all, no. I mean, the reason is pretty clear to me. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, that’s when I started feeling this way.

It’s not like I’m falling apart during the school day—I’m still there, still teaching, still doing what has to be done in front of the students—but inside I feel tense and kind of discouraged in a way that really doesn’t feel like me.\n\nIt’s mostly tied to the extra responsibility the district put on me, the bilingual social-emotional learning program on top of my regular teaching. I’ll think, okay, I need to adjust these lesson plans, I need to answer those emails, I need to stay on top of the rollout materials, and instead of just doing it like I normally would, I put it off. So yes, physically or emotionally—maybe both—I feel keyed up around it, even though I can still get through the day.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about other areas of your life, like relationships, health, or everyday responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, it’s like I can still get through the school day and do what I need to do in front of the students, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nOutside of work, it’s more that the work feeling follows me home, if that makes sense. It’s really centered around this added responsibility at school.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting the same satisfaction or enjoyment out of things you usually like to do, even outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost enjoyment in things, exactly. I mean, I haven’t sat down and thought, “Oh, I don’t like this anymore,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more that when I’m outside of work, I’m often still sort of mentally tangled up in the work piece, so I may not settle into things as easily.\n\nBut I wouldn’t want to overstate it. If I’m doing something I usually enjoy, I can still enjoy it. It’s just that sometimes there’s this background noise in my head about what I haven’t finished or what I need to respond to, and that makes it harder to feel fully relaxed. So… not no enjoyment, really. More like my attention gets pulled back toward school.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or losing interest in things more often, even small things that used to feel rewarding or give you a sense of accomplishment?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s like I’ve lost interest across the board, but yes, I do feel more discouraged than I’m used to, and it’s mostly around this work situation. I came in because ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate—on top of my regular teaching—I can still get through the day and do what I need to do, but I feel wound up and discouraged in a way that just isn’t like me.\n\nAnd the part that bothers me is that small work things that used to give me a sense of, “Okay, that’s done, I’m on top of it,” don’t feel that way right now because I’m putting them off. Lesson plans, emails, rollout materials—those are things I used to handle without a second thought. Now I’ll know I need to do them, and I’ll avoid them, and then I feel worse because that’s not how I usually operate. So it’s not that nothing feels rewarding anymore, exactly. It’s more that this added responsibility has made me feel kind of stuck and discouraged, and even little accomplishments at work don’t come as naturally as they used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood most of the time, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Not most of the time, no. I mean, I do feel discouraged, and more than I’m used to, but when I really look at it, it keeps circling back to the school situation. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate on top of my regular teaching, I can still get through the day and be present for the students, but I feel wound up and kind of defeated in a way that doesn’t feel like me.\n\nWhen I’m not thinking about work, I don’t think I’m just sitting in a low mood all the time. It’s more like work intrudes. I’ll be at home and suddenly remember lesson plans I haven’t adjusted, or emails I haven’t answered, and then I get that heavy, discouraged feeling because those are things I used to handle without much thought. Now I’m putting them off, and that makes me feel worse about myself.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m low even completely separate from work. It’s more that this work stress has started spilling over, and I’m having trouble shaking it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it hard to control your worry, even when you try, or noticed that it’s starting to interfere with your ability to focus or get things done outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s the part I’m having the hardest time admitting, honestly. I’m still functioning. I’m still showing up, still teaching, still doing the day-to-day things I absolutely have to do. So from the outside, I probably look like I’m managing.\n\nBut I’m not managing the way I normally do. I’ve pushed back planning deadlines, or just sort of missed the internal deadlines I would usually set for myself. And there have been a few times where colleagues have had to remind me about things—small tasks, planning pieces, materials, follow-ups—that I used to just take care of reliably. That feels very unlike me.\n\nIt’s not that I’m completely unable to work or anything like that. It’s more that the worry keeps taking up space in my head, and then I’ll avoid starting something, or I’ll jump between things and not finish them. Then someone checks in or reminds me, and I feel embarrassed because I’m usually the person who has those things handled. So yes, it has started interfering, even if I’m still keeping the basic responsibilities going.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that activities you usually enjoy don’t feel rewarding or pleasurable anymore, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that broad way you’re asking. I mean, I have moments where I’m distracted, so something I’d normally enjoy might not fully land because my mind drifts back to school. But I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone from things in general.\n\nIf I’m really not thinking about work, I can still enjoy things. It’s more that getting myself fully away from thinking about work has been harder. So the activity itself isn’t the problem, if that makes sense. It’s not like I sit down to do something I normally like and feel absolutely nothing. It’s more that I’ll be trying to relax and then a thought about some task or email or planning piece pops in, and it sort of interrupts the enjoyment.\n\nSo no, I haven’t noticed that everything outside of work feels unrewarding. It’s more tied to whether I can actually get my head out of work mode.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a lasting loss of interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even when you're not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it a lasting loss, no. At least, not the way I understand what you’re asking. I still do have interest in things I normally enjoy, and if I’m actually able to be present with them, they can still feel good.\n\nIt’s more that my attention gets pulled away, if that makes sense. Like I might start doing something I usually like and then I realize I’m half there, half somewhere else mentally. But it doesn’t feel like the enjoyment itself is gone. It feels more like I’m not always giving myself enough mental space to enjoy it.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed a steady, across-the-board loss of interest or pleasure when work isn’t on my mind. If I’m truly not thinking about work, I can still enjoy things.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood even when you’re not thinking about work or any of the added responsibilities at school?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I feel low in a completely separate, out-of-nowhere way. It feels tied to all of this school responsibility, even when I’m not actively sitting there thinking, “Oh, the rollout, the emails, the lesson plans.” It’s like the pressure has followed me around.\n\nEver since the district put that bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt… wound up, and honestly pretty discouraged in a way that doesn’t feel like me. I still get through the day. I’m not falling apart in front of my class or anything. But there’s this heaviness or discouragement that’s been there because I know I’m not keeping up the way I usually do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off things like lesson plans or emails—things I used to just handle without a second thought—and that makes me feel worse about myself. So yes, I guess I do feel down at times, but I see it as connected to this added responsibility and the stress around school, not like a general low mood that’s totally unrelated to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’ve lost interest in almost all activities, even ones you used to enjoy, even temporarily?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve lost interest in almost all activities. Even temporarily, I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly.\n\nThere are times when I sort of don’t feel like doing much, but when I really think about it, it’s usually because I’m preoccupied or tired from having my mind on school things. It’s not that the activities themselves suddenly seem meaningless or like I don’t care about them anymore. If I can settle into something, I can still enjoy it.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is no, not almost all activities. It’s more like my attention gets hijacked, rather than my interest disappearing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you’re not thinking about work, things you used to enjoy feel less satisfying or take more effort to get into?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed that in the way you mean. I mean, I can think of moments where something takes me longer to settle into, but I don’t know that it’s because the thing itself feels less satisfying. It’s more like I’m a little… distracted or not fully shifted out of “school mode,” even if I’m not consciously thinking through work.\n\nLike, if I sit down to do something I normally like, I might need a few minutes to actually feel present. But once I’m there, it can still feel enjoyable. So I wouldn’t say the enjoyment is gone or that things consistently feel dull or empty. It’s more that I don’t always transition into them as easily right now.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is: not really less satisfying, no. Maybe sometimes a little more effort to get into, but not because I’ve stopped caring about them.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you feel like nothing gives you pleasure or interest, even things you normally enjoy, and this lasts for most of the day, even when work isn’t on your mind?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not in that “nothing gives me pleasure” kind of way, and not for most of a day.\n\nI mean, there are stretches where I’m kind of dulled out or I’m not as quick to reach for something enjoyable, if that makes sense. Like I might not immediately think, “Oh, I want to do this nice thing,” because my mind feels crowded or I’m just not very settled. But if I’m actually doing something I normally like, I can still get something out of it. It may take me a little while to shift gears, but I wouldn’t say the pleasure is absent.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed whole days where everything feels pointless or flat even when work isn’t on my mind. It’s more uneven than that, and it still feels connected to being distracted or weighed down by the school situation rather than losing interest in life in general.\n\nDoctor: Outside of thinking about work or the added responsibilities, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried most days, even about small or everyday things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say that, no. I do worry, but it feels pretty tied to the work situation and the extra responsibilities, not like I’m just anxious about everything across the board.\n\nLike, if I’m making coffee or running errands or talking to someone about something unrelated, I don’t usually find myself suddenly worrying about random small things for no reason. It’s more that if something reminds me of school, or I start thinking about what still needs to be handled, then my mind can start going. But outside of that, I’m not generally walking around feeling anxious most days about ordinary little things.\n\nI mean, I may get a little tense the way anyone does if there’s traffic or I’m running late or something, but I wouldn’t call that this constant worry. So… no, not most days about everyday things apart from work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities, like in general or during routine daily activities?\n\nPatient: I think I’d answer that pretty much the same way, though I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to sound like I’m dismissing it. I do get tense, but I don’t really notice myself being anxious or on edge in a general way when work isn’t somewhere in the picture.\n\nLike if I’m just doing normal things—folding laundry, going to the store, making dinner, driving somewhere familiar—I’m not usually sitting there with this background hum of worry for no clear reason. If I feel keyed up, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to school, or something reminds me of what I need to deal with, or I realize I forgot to check something. Then I can feel that little surge of tension.\n\nBut if it’s truly not on my mind, no, I don’t think I’m generally anxious during routine activities. I might be tired or distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as being on edge all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood for most of the day, more days than not, even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I mean, I can feel kind of low or discouraged at times, but when I really think about what you’re asking—most of the day, more days than not, and not connected to thinking about work or all the added responsibilities—I don’t think that fits.\n\nIt’s more like my mood dips when something brings the situation back up, or when I’m mentally going over everything and feeling worn down by it. I mean, the reason is pretty clear to me. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, that’s when I started feeling this way.\n\nI still get through the day—I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely falling apart—but I feel wound up in a way that doesn’t feel like me. And discouraged, too. Like I’ll look at lesson plans or emails, things I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it, and now I catch myself putting them off because I just feel this tension about it.\n\nSo if I’m anxious, it usually has that attached to it. It’s not like I’m sitting at home worrying out of nowhere about random things. It’s more like there’s this specific load that got added, and it’s been wearing on me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I’d say I don’t care about things anymore. That sounds a little stronger than what I’ve noticed.

There are definitely evenings where I don’t feel like doing much, and if someone asked me, “Do you want to go do this thing you normally enjoy?” I might say no just because I feel kind of worn out or preoccupied. So… no, not most days about everyday things apart from work.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or on edge even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities, like in general or during routine daily activities?\n\nPatient: I think I’d answer that pretty much the same way, though I’m trying to be careful because I don’t want to sound like I’m dismissing it. I do get tense, but I don’t really notice myself being anxious or on edge in a general way when work isn’t somewhere in the picture.\n\nLike if I’m just doing normal things—folding laundry, going to the store, making dinner, driving somewhere familiar—I’m not usually sitting there with this background hum of worry for no clear reason. If I feel keyed up, it’s usually because my mind has drifted back to school, or something reminds me of what I need to deal with, or I realize I forgot to check something. Then I can feel that little surge of tension.\n\nBut if it’s truly not on my mind, no, I don’t think I’m generally anxious during routine activities. I might be tired or distracted sometimes, but I wouldn’t describe it as being on edge all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or in a low mood for most of the day, more days than not, even when you’re not thinking about work or the added responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. I mean, I can feel kind of low or discouraged at times, but when I really think about what you’re asking—most of the day, more days than not, and not connected to thinking about work or all the added responsibilities—I don’t think that fits.\n\nIt’s more like my mood dips when something brings the situation back up, or when I’m mentally going over everything and feeling worn down by it. But if I’m away from it, or absorbed in something else, I’m not generally just low all day for no reason. I wouldn’t say I’m cheerful all the time or anything, but it doesn’t feel like this steady low mood that’s there regardless of what I’m thinking about.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed feeling down most of the day on most days outside of that context.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself feeling anxious or worried even when there’s no clear reason or specific problem on your mind?\n\nPatient: Not really with no reason at all, no. I mean, the reason is pretty clear to me. Ever since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, that’s when I started feeling this way.\n\nI still get through the day—I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely falling apart—but I feel wound up in a way that doesn’t feel like me. And discouraged, too. Like I’ll look at lesson plans or emails, things I used to just handle without making a whole production out of it, and now I catch myself putting them off because I just feel this tension about it.\n\nSo if I’m anxious, it usually has that attached to it. It’s not like I’m sitting at home worrying out of nowhere about random things. It’s more like there’s this specific load that got added, and it’s been wearing on me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not interested in doing things you usually like, even temporarily, like you just don’t care to do them anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I don’t care about things anymore. That sounds a little stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nThere are definitely evenings where I don’t feel like doing much, and if someone asked me, “Do you want to go do this thing you normally enjoy?” I might say no just because I feel kind of worn out or preoccupied. But it doesn’t feel like the interest is gone, exactly. It’s more like I don’t always have the mental energy to start something.\n\nLike, once I’m actually doing something I usually like, I can still enjoy it. I haven’t had that feeling of, “What’s the point, I don’t like anything anymore,” if that’s what you mean. It’s more temporary, and usually tied to feeling drained. So… no, I don’t think I’ve lost interest in things in that bigger way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
